Troubleshooting

Device shows “Disconnected”:

  1. Check if Syncthing is running on the remote device (brew services list | grep syncthing or systemctl status syncthing@root)
  2. Verify the device address is set to tcp://IP:22000 (not just dynamic)
  3. Check UFW on Proxmox: ufw status | grep -E '22000|21027'
  4. Restart Syncthing: brew services restart syncthing or systemctl restart syncthing@root

Files not syncing:

  1. Check the Syncthing web UI for errors or conflicts
  2. Verify the folder path exists on both devices
  3. Check folder type is “Send & Receive” on all devices
  4. Look for .stignore files that might be filtering content

Conflict files:

Syncthing creates .sync-conflict-YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS files when the same file is modified on multiple devices simultaneously. Resolve by keeping the correct version and deleting the conflict copy.

Reset a device’s Syncthing config:

# Mac (will regenerate on next start)
brew services stop syncthing
rm -rf ~/Library/Application\ Support/Syncthing/
brew services start syncthing

# Proxmox
systemctl stop syncthing@root
rm -rf /root/.local/state/syncthing/
systemctl start syncthing@root

After resetting, you’ll need to re-add devices and shared folders.

Background & Decision Log

Problem: Typinator and BetterTouchTool configuration files were not syncing reliably between Mac Studio and MacBook despite using their built-in iCloud sync. iCloud Drive was identified as the root cause — it aggressively evicts files to free local storage, handles frequently-updated small files poorly, and creates silent conflict copies.

Solution: Syncthing was deployed with Proxmox as the always-on hub. This provides: real-time LAN sync without cloud dependency, no file eviction, proper conflict detection with visible conflict files, and ZFS-backed storage on the hub with automatic snapshots every 15 minutes.

Why not direct Mac-to-Mac sync: Both Macs would need to be powered on simultaneously for sync to occur. With Proxmox as the hub, one Mac can be off — changes queue on Proxmox and sync when the other Mac comes online.

Installed: March 27, 2026.

WireGuard Tunnel — CT100 → UltraCC

Deployed April 29, 2026 on CT100. Replaces the per-app SOCKS5 proxy approach for outbound privacy on torrent indexers, Hardcover lookups, etc.

What this does and why

Before: Shelfmark and the *arr stack used a SOCKS5 proxy (autossh tunnel to seedbox at port 1080) for outbound traffic that needed to look non-residential. Each app had to be individually configured.

After: A WireGuard interface on CT100 (wg0) routes all outbound from every container through UltraCC’s NL endpoint. Apps don’t need any per-service proxy config; they just talk to the internet normally and the tunnel handles the rest.
Tunnel endpoint UltraCC NL — 45.86.221.26:13012
Server pubkey bOBo86jrNKBikLqOFdksL41bTnQHWWHynM3/v3yq2Vg=
Client (CT100) IP 10.13.13.2/32
Listen port 51820 (CT100 side)
AllowedIPs 0.0.0.0/0 (full tunnel)
MTU 1420
Public IP from CT100 45.86.221.26 (NL exit)

Private key + PSK for this tunnel were passed through chat — rotation pending. Replace the peer in the UltraCC panel and re-import the config to rotate.

Topology
Container (e.g., sonarr)
    │ HTTPS to indexer.com
    ▼
Docker bridge 172.16.x.0/24
    │ (MASQUERADE in nat POSTROUTING)
    ▼
CT100 routing table
    │ default via wg0 (table 51820, fwmark 0xca6c)
    ▼
wg0 — WireGuard interface
    │ encrypted UDP to 45.86.221.26:13012
    ▼
CT100 eth0 → OPNsense → ISP → UltraCC NL
    │
    ▼
Public internet (source IP appears as 45.86.221.26)

LAN traffic (192.168.8.0/24, 172.16.0.0/12, 100.123.0.0/16) bypasses the tunnel. Only outbound to the public internet goes through wg0.

Kill-switch

The pre-existing CT100 firewall acts as the kill-switch — if wg0 drops, the OUTPUT chain has no rules permitting public-internet egress, so all packets are dropped (with explicit LOG+DROP rule at the end). No traffic leaks to the ISP if the tunnel fails.

Permitted egress (whitelist in /etc/iptables/rules.v4):

  • 192.168.8.0/24 — LAN
  • 172.16.0.0/12 — Docker bridges
  • 100.123.0.0/16 — NetBird overlay
  • DNS to 192.168.8.53 — Pi-hole
  • SSH to 46.232.210.50:22 — seedbox (for autossh tunnels still in use)
  • UDP to 45.86.221.26:13012 — WireGuard handshake to UltraCC
  • Anything via wg0 (the tunnel itself)

Everything else: LOG + DROP.

MSS clamping (critical)

WireGuard’s MTU (1420) is smaller than Docker bridges (1500). Without MSS clamping, TCP from containers fails on TLS handshake — packets get fragmented or silently dropped, connections hang. Symptoms: ping works (small packets) but curl https://... times out.

Fix is in the mangle table:

iptables -t mangle -A FORWARD -o wg0 -p tcp --tcp-flags SYN,RST SYN \
    -j TCPMSS --clamp-mss-to-pmtu
iptables -t mangle -A POSTROUTING -o wg0 -p tcp --tcp-flags SYN,RST SYN \
    -j TCPMSS --clamp-mss-to-pmtu

Both rules are persisted in /etc/iptables/rules.v4.

Systemd & boot persistence
# Enabled on CT100:
systemctl is-enabled wg-quick@wg0    # → enabled
systemctl status wg-quick@wg0        # → active

/etc/wireguard/wg0.conf holds the peer config (chmod 600, root-only). On boot, wg-quick@wg0.service brings up the interface, applies the iptables rules in /etc/iptables/rules.v4 via iptables-restore, and the kill-switch + MSS clamp + tunnel are all live.

Health checks & diagnostics

Verify tunnel from CT100:

pct exec 100 -- wg show wg0
# Expect: handshake within last 2-3 minutes, transfer counters incrementing.

pct exec 100 -- curl -s https://api.ipify.org
# Expect: 45.86.221.26 (NOT your home WAN IP).

Verify from a container (e.g., Sonarr):

pct exec 100 -- docker exec sonarr sh -c \
    'curl -sS -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code} via %{remote_ip}" https://api.ipify.org'
# Expect: 200 via <some-ipify-IP>; the response body shows 45.86.221.26

Uptime Kuma monitor (id 76, “WireGuard Exit IP (UltraCC)”) does this exact check every 5 minutes — keyword 45.86.221.26 against https://api.ipify.org. Alerts via Gotify if the tunnel drops or routing changes.

If TCP fails but ping works: MSS clamp rules are missing (see MSS clamping).

If the whole tunnel is dead: check for handshake errors. Often the cure is systemctl restart wg-quick@wg0. If UltraCC re-keyed, you’ll need a fresh config from their panel.

Containers using the tunnel

All of them — every CT100 container’s outbound traffic exits via 45.86.221.26. This is by design.

The WireGuard tunnel replaced the SOCKS5 proxy (autossh-socks / seedbox-socks) for outbound privacy routing — apps no longer need per-service proxy configuration. However, the SSH RPC tunnels are NOT redundant and remain actively required:

Tunnel Purpose Status
transmission-tunnel.service (hpve) SSH tunnel :13010 → seedbox Transmission RPC. Used by Sonarr/Radarr/Lidarr for download management Active — required
autossh-transmission.service (CT100) AutoSSH tunnel :13010 → seedbox Transmission RPC Active — required
autossh-nzbget.service (CT100) AutoSSH tunnel :16789 → seedbox NZBGet Active — required
autossh-socks.service (CT100) SOCKS5 :1080 → seedbox NL exit Active — legacy, replaced by WireGuard for most apps

WireGuard handles privacy routing (making outbound traffic appear from NL); it does not replace the RPC control tunnels that let the *arr apps communicate with download clients on the seedbox.

Backup of installation state

When the tunnel was deployed, a snapshot was taken at:

/root/wg-deploy-backup-20260429-150327/
  rules.v4.before    # iptables before WG
  iptables.live.before

To roll back: iptables-restore < /root/wg-deploy-backup-*/rules.v4.before && systemctl stop wg-quick@wg0 && systemctl disable wg-quick@wg0.

Version history and our project checklist

Keeping this reference accurate

When work changes a documented system, update its page in the same task. Preserve the reason for deliberate decisions, clearly retire superseded behavior, and remove contradictory instructions rather than only appending a new note.

Separate three kinds of evidence:

  • Observed: a current configuration, log, read-back, or real workflow test, with its date.
  • Documented/reported: a dated prior record or Ed’s statement; not automatically live state.
  • Planned/unverified: a proposed design or an unresolved condition.

last_substantive_update records a meaningful edit. It does not imply that every service mentioned on the page was re-tested. Fresh generated registries can also contain incomplete checks; exit status alone is not proof of a useful result.

Private working notes stay outside the published tree. Bee Hub’s public and internal copies share a build, so credentials, private feed URLs, and personal records must not be added to publishable pages.

Documentation maintenance is part of subsequent work; no new continuous watcher was created. Some older pages still need reconciliation against current evidence.

Zigbee & Z-Wave — Farm

The farm’s buildings are hundreds of feet apart, so a single Zigbee mesh cannot span them — Zigbee routers can only relay to neighbours they can hear. The answer is four completely independent Zigbee networks, one per building, each with its own Ethernet-attached coordinator and its own Zigbee2MQTT instance, all reporting into the one Home Assistant at 192.168.0.10.

Built 2026-08-22. Every value on this page was read from the live configuration, not from notes.

Architecture

Each building has an SLZB-series Zigbee coordinator on PoE Ethernet, not USB. Zigbee2MQTT talks to it over TCP (serial.port: tcp://<ip>:<port>, adapter zstack) rather than a serial device — which is what makes the coordinator’s physical location independent of where Home Assistant runs.

Home Assistant runs four Zigbee2MQTT add-ons: the standard one plus three “Multi-Instance” copies. They are separate add-ons with separate data directories and separate MQTT base topics, so nothing is shared but the Mosquitto broker.

Cattle Barn SLZB ──┐
House SLZB ────────┤   Ethernet / PoE     ┌── Z2M (base)      → zigbee2mqtt/…
Equipment SLZB ────┼─────────────────────►├── Z2M MI N°1      → zigbee2mqtt_house/…
Stable SLZB ───────┘                      ├── Z2M MI N°2      → zigbee2mqtt_equipment/…
                                          └── Z2M MI N°3      → zigbee2mqtt_stable/…
                                                    │
                                            Mosquitto broker
                                                    │
                                            Home Assistant
The Four Networks
Building Add-on Coordinator Channel PAN ID MQTT base topic Data path
Cattle Barn Zigbee2MQTT (45df7312_zigbee2mqtt) tcp://192.168.0.19:6638 20 6420 zigbee2mqtt /config/zigbee2mqtt
House Multi-Instance N°1 (099a1f1b_zigbee2mqtt_mi_1) tcp://192.168.0.100:7638 25 44144 zigbee2mqtt_house /config/zigbee2mqtt_house
Equipment Barn Multi-Instance N°2 (099a1f1b_zigbee2mqtt_mi_2) tcp://192.168.0.28:6638 15 50674 zigbee2mqtt_equipment /config/zigbee2mqtt_equipment
Stable Multi-Instance N°3 (099a1f1b_zigbee2mqtt_mi_3) tcp://192.168.0.18:6638 26 51032 zigbee2mqtt_stable /config/zigbee2mqtt_stable

All four run Zigbee2MQTT 2.13.0-1, adapter zstack, rtscts: false, frontend on internal port 8099 reached through HA ingress, log_level: info, enable_external_js: false.

Note the House coordinator answers on 7638, not 6638 like the other three. That is not a typo — check it before “fixing” it.

Bridge devices in Home Assistant (all manufacturer: Zigbee2MQTT, model: Bridge):

Bridge device Area Coordinator IEEE
Cattle Barn Zigbee Bridge Cattle Barn 0x00124b002a76160d
House Zigbee Bridge House 0x00124b00341392c0
Equipment Barn Zigbee Bridge Equipment Barn 0x00124b002a7616f5
Stable Zigbee Bridge Stable 0x00124b002d12c323

Each bridge contributes 8 entities (connection state, restart button, permit-join, log level, version, etc.) — 32 in total, renamed to a <building>_zigbee_* scheme and area-assigned.

Network keys, extended PAN IDs and the MQTT broker password are not recorded here. They live in the per-instance configuration.yaml on the HA box and in SECRETS.md.

Channel Plan — and Why It Matters

Zigbee and 2.4 GHz WiFi share the same band. Each Zigbee channel sits under a specific slice of WiFi spectrum, so the plan is to keep the four Zigbee networks apart from each other and out from under the busiest WiFi channels.

Zigbee channel Sits under Assigned to Verdict
15 Gap between WiFi 1 and 6 Equipment Barn Good
20 Gap between WiFi 6 and 11 Cattle Barn Good
25 Just above WiFi 11 House Very good
26 Above the entire WiFi band (2480 MHz) Stable Best — clear regardless of what WiFi does

Which zigbee2mqtt instance is which (verified 2026-08-22 by reading each configuration.yaml on the HA box — the add-ons are named “Zigbee2MQTT” and “Zigbee2MQTT multi-instance №1/2/3”, which tells you nothing, and all four use frontend port 8099 internally so the port does not disambiguate either). Identify an instance by its coordinator IP or MQTT base topic, both visible in the z2m UI:

Barn / area Config dir MQTT base topic Coordinator Channel
Cattle Barn /config/zigbee2mqtt zigbee2mqtt 192.168.0.19:6638 20
Equipment Barn /config/zigbee2mqtt_equipment zigbee2mqtt_equipment 192.168.0.28:6638 15
House /config/zigbee2mqtt_house zigbee2mqtt_house 192.168.0.100:7638 25
Stable /config/zigbee2mqtt_stable zigbee2mqtt_stable 192.168.0.18:6638 26

Note the trap: Cattle Barn is the base zigbee2mqtt instance, not a _cattle one. /config/zigbee2mqtt_cattle exists but is empty and vestigial — no configuration.yaml, no channel, no devices. Do not configure anything there. /config/zigbee2mqtt.retired-20260611 is also dead.

Easier route — skip the add-on UI entirely. The HA entities are named by barn, so pairing can be driven from Home Assistant or the API without ever identifying an add-on number: switch.<house|stable|equipment|cattle_barn>_zigbee_permit_join, binary_sensor.*_zigbee_connection_state, button.*_zigbee_restart. Helper: ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/zigbee-pair.py <network> --open|--watch|--close, which snapshots the entity list first so a newly joined device is obvious. Inventory check: ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/zigbee-devices.py.

These four are the standard WiFi-avoiding set. The Stable was moved from channel 11 to 26 on 2026-08-22 — channel 11 sits directly under WiFi channel 1, and with zero devices paired at the time the move was free. Channel 26 has a slightly lower regulatory TX-power ceiling on some CC2652 radios; at Stable-building distances that is not a practical limit.

The WiFi side is now pinned too (2026-08-22). Previously six of the nine 2.4 GHz radios sat on channel: "0" — Auto RF — so the “sits under” column above was only guaranteed for Zigbee 26. Every 2.4 GHz radio is now fixed to 1, 6 or 11, which makes the three narrow gaps that Zigbee 15/20/25 depend on permanent rather than incidental.

AP IP 2.4 GHz channel
House Inside AP 192.168.0.8 1
House Outside AP 192.168.0.4 11
Stable Indoor AP 192.168.0.12 6
Stable Outdoor AP 192.168.0.32 1
Cattle Indoor AP 192.168.0.37 still Auto — will not accept a manual channel, see below
Cattle AP Outdoor 192.168.0.25 6
Equipment Indoor AP 192.168.0.27 6
Equipment Outdoor AP 192.168.0.31 1
Meadow 192.168.0.14 11
Meadow Bridge 192.168.0.23 no 2.4 GHz radio (5 GHz backhaul only)

Indoor and outdoor radios at the same building are deliberately on different channels; reuse across buildings is fine at farm distances. Auto RF bought nothing here — there are no neighbouring networks to dodge — while leaving the Zigbee plan able to break silently.

Tooling: ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/omada-radio.py (read, --backup, --plan, --apply). Pre-change state is saved at ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/omada-radios-backup-20260822.json; the assignment lives in ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/omada-channel-plan.json. Reverting = set every channel to 0 and re-apply.

API gotcha — silent no-op writes. PATCH /{omadacId}/api/v2/sites/{site}/eaps/{mac} must carry only the fields being changed. Echoing the whole radioSetting2g object back is accepted with errorCode: 0 and then ignored, because that object contains freq, and freq: 0 means “auto” and outranks channel. Six of seven writes no-opped that way with no error of any kind. Send {"radioSetting2g": {"channel": "11"}} alone and the controller derives freq itself (channel 11 → freq: 2462, which also confirms the value is a real channel number, not a list index — unlike the 5 GHz channel field, whose values are indexes).

There is a site-level override: RF Planning / Wireless Optimization. GET /{omadacId}/api/v2/sites/{site}/rfPlanning shows it enabled and scheduled daily at 00:00, with channelDeployEnable2g, channelWidthDeployEnable, bandDeployEnable and powerAdjustEnable all true. It re-deploys channel, width and power to every AP not on its excludeAps list. Seven APs are on that list; the three that are not are House Inside AP, Cattle Indoor AP and Equipment Indoor AP — so a pinned channel on any of those three is liable to be overwritten overnight. Of the three only Equipment Indoor is both live and successfully pinned, so that is the one at real risk.

Writing the exclusion list over the API does not work: every write method on /rfPlanning returns -1600 Unsupported request path, and PUT /rfPlanning/schedule returns success while silently ignoring excludeAps (read-back unchanged) — the same accepted-and-ignored trap as the freq field above. Change exclusions in the UI on the “AI RF Planning” page, in the wireless-networks settings group (siblings: WiFi Network, WLAN Schedule, MAC Filter, MAC Group; a “Planning History” tab sits beside it). That name is read from the controller’s own view template modules/settings/wireless_networks/ai_rf_planning/view-b404ec4dda.html, which renders exactly two i18n keys — WIRELESS_NETWORKS.AI_RF_PLANING and WIRELESS_NETWORKS.HISTORY_PLANING. It is not called “Wireless Optimization” or “WLAN Optimization”; wlan_optimization_v2 is only the backend permission key. Whether excludeAps is even user-editable is unconfirmed; each entry carries an unsupportType field, which hints it may be computed. Inspector: ~/Sync/ED/scripts/ha-omada/omada-rfplanning.py, backup omada-rfplanning-backup-20260822.json.

Cattle Indoor AP will not take a manual channel. In the UI its channel dropdown reads “no channels available” — confirmed by Ed on 2026-08-22, and exactly what an empty legal-channel list looks like. This is a separate problem from RF Planning. It accepts the write, returns success, and stays on Auto. It is the only AP on the site reporting deviceMisc.customizeRegion: 0 (every other AP reports 841) along with maxPower2G: 0 and txPower: 0 — no regulatory domain is loaded, so the controller has no legal channel or power table to validate against. It also runs firmware 1.1.1 (March 2025) while the identical EAP670 in the Stable runs 1.3.11 (July 2026), and its MAC (00-0A-EB-13-7B-00) is in a Tenda OUI rather than a TP-Link one. Firmware upgrade or a forget-and-re-adopt is the likely fix; both are physical-access risks, so neither was attempted remotely.

The coordinator_backup.json Channel-Change Trap

Changing advanced.channel in configuration.yaml is not enough. Zigbee2MQTT restores the radio’s network parameters — including the channel — from coordinator_backup.json on startup. Edit only the YAML and the coordinator quietly comes back up on the old channel, with no error in the log.

To actually move a network:

  1. Stop the Zigbee2MQTT instance.
  2. Move coordinator_backup.json aside (do not delete — it is the network’s identity).
  3. Change advanced.channel in configuration.yaml.
  4. Start the instance. Z2M re-forms on the new channel and writes a fresh backup.
  5. Every battery-powered end device must be re-paired. Routers usually follow the coordinator; sleepy end devices generally do not.

Two instances carry the evidence of exactly this operation: Cattle Barn has coordinator_backup.json.pre-ch20 / database.db.pre-ch20, and Stable has configuration.yaml.pre-ch26 / coordinator_backup.json.pre-ch26 / database.db.pre-ch26 from the 2026-08-22 move. Keep that naming convention for future channel moves.

The 2026-08-22 Stable move is a clean worked example. The log said exactly what you want to see:

zh:controller: Configured channel '26' does not match adapter channel '11', changing channel
zh:controller: Changing channel from '11' to '26'
zh:controller: Channel changed to '26'
zh:controller: Wrote coordinator backup to '/config/zigbee2mqtt_stable/coordinator_backup.json'

Note the ~50-second gap between “Channel changed” and “Wrote coordinator backup” — the add-on looks hung during that window. It is not. Wait it out.

HA integration zwave_js, connected over the Z-Wave JS UI WebSocket server
Nodes paired 1 — the controller only. No devices included yet.

The Z-Wave JS UI WebSocket server has to be explicitly enabled in the add-on before the zwave_js integration will connect; it is off by default. Z-Wave is a single network for the whole property (unlike Zigbee), which is why Long Range matters here — the buildings are far apart.

Operating Rules

Watchdog ON, auto-update OFF across all four Zigbee2MQTT instances, Z-Wave JS UI and Mosquitto. The watchdog restarts a crashed add-on; auto-update is off because a Z2M or Z-Wave JS major version can require a migration, and an unattended 3 a.m. upgrade that breaks the radio takes the whole farm’s sensing offline.

Monitoring — Zigbee Bridge Offline Alert (HA automation, added 2026-08-22). Watches all four binary_sensor.*_zigbee_connection_state entities; fires on off or unavailable for 10 minutes, to iPhone push plus a persistent notification with a per-bridge notification_id so repeats replace in place. Mode queued, max 4.

This automation exists because the Cattle Barn bridge sat dead from 2026-08-15 to 2026-08-22 and nobody noticed. A silently dead coordinator looks exactly like a quiet building.

Monitoring — Zigbee Coordinator Overheating (HA automation, added 2026-08-22). The SLZB units report their own core and radio chip temperatures through the smlight integration, and nothing was watching them. Fires above 165 °F sustained for 30 minutes on any of the nine temperature sensors, to iPhone push plus a per-sensor persistent notification.

Baseline readings at the time the automation was written — same hardware class, same PoE, wildly different results:

Coordinator Core chip Radio chip
Equipment Barn 155 °F 152 °F
Stable 136 °F 129 °F
Cattle Barn 116 °F 112 °F
House (MR-W10U) 98 °F 94 °F

The Equipment Barn unit is the outlier by a wide margin and Stable is second. That is an enclosure, airflow or sun-exposure problem, not a firmware one. The 165 °F threshold is set deliberately above Equipment’s resting value so the alert means “getting worse”, not “still hot”. Live chart: Trends → Infrastructure in Home Assistant.

Do not enable permit_join and walk away. Each bridge has its own permit-join control; leaving one open indefinitely is how a device ends up on the wrong building’s network.

Pairing goes to the nearest building’s network. There is no roaming between the four — a device paired to Cattle Barn will not work in the Stable.

Gotchas
Symptom Cause
Channel change “didn’t take” coordinator_backup.json restored the old channel — see above
Bridge shows unavailable in HA but the SLZB web UI is up Z2M lost its TCP session to the coordinator; restart the add-on, not the coordinator
House coordinator “wrong port” House genuinely uses 7638; the other three use 6638
A device paired but appears under the wrong building Wrong Z2M instance had permit-join open
Watchman reports a Z2M entity missing after a rename Bridge entity renames do not propagate to dashboards; search and update card references
SLZB running hot Equipment Barn’s coordinator measured 154 °F core vs 111 °F (Cattle Barn) and 97 °F (House) on identical hardware — enclosure/airflow/sun exposure issue, check before loading it with a live network
Where Things Live
Thing Location
Z2M configs /config/zigbee2mqtt{,_house,_equipment,_stable}/configuration.yaml on the HA box
Z2M databases database.db in the same directories
Coordinator backups coordinator_backup.json in the same directories
SLZB web UIs http://192.168.0.19, .100, .28, .18
Z2M frontends Home Assistant → Settings → Add-ons → (instance) → Open Web UI (ingress)
Z-Wave JS UI Home Assistant sidebar (ingress panel)
MQTT broker Mosquitto add-on, mqtt://core-mosquitto:1883
Secrets ~/Sync/ED/Files/SECRETS.md

Shell access to the HA box: ssh ha (user delgross, key ~/.ssh/ha_id_ed25519). The .storage and add-on config directories are root-owned — use sudo.

Never leave .bak files inside /homeassistant/.storage/. Watchman parses every file in that directory, so a backup copy of a dashboard makes every entity in it get re-reported as missing. Put backups somewhere else — e.g. /root/.
